. Hence, we can assume that these terms do not have any effect on the system and we can neglect them, thus the Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H^{\textrm{I}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{1}\nu_{1}(be^{-i\nu_{1}t}+b^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{1}t})\sigma_{1}^{z}-\eta_{2}\nu_{2}(ce^{-i\nu_{2}t}+c^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{2}t})\sigma_{1}^{z}

(124)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\eta_{1}\nu_{1}(be^{-i\nu_{1}t}+b^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{1}t})\sigma_{2}^{z}+\eta_{2}\nu_{2}(ce^{-i\nu_{2}t}+c^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{2}t})\sigma_{2}^{z}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{1}(t)}{2}(\sigma_{1}^{+}e^{i\phi}+\sigma_{1}^{-}e^{-i\phi})+\frac{\Omega_{2}(t)}{2}(\sigma_{2}^{+}e^{i\phi}+\sigma_{2}^{-}e^{-i\phi}),

which corresponds to Eq. (
12
) in the main text.

Appendix 7.D
The time-evolution operator

An analytical expression for the time evolution operator exits for any Hamiltonian of the form

\displaystyle H^{\textrm{II}}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{M}f_{j}(t)\eta_{jm}\nu_{m}(a_{m}e^{-i\nu_{m}t}+a_{m}^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{m}t})\sigma_{j}^{z}.

(125)

The Hamiltonian for our ion system, undergoing a sequence of instantaneous
\pi
-pulses, is given by

\displaystyle H^{\textrm{I}I}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{1}(t)\eta_{1}\nu_{1}(be^{-i\nu_{1}t}+b^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{1}t})\ \sigma_{1}^{z}

(126)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle f_{1}(t)\eta_{2}\nu_{2}(ce^{-i\nu_{2}t}+c^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{2}t})\ \sigma_{1}^{z}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle f_{2}(t)\eta_{1}\nu_{1}(be^{-i\nu_{1}t}+b^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{1}t})\ \sigma_{2}^{z}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle f_{2}(t)\eta_{2}\nu_{2}(ce^{-i\nu_{2}t}+c^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{2}t})\ \sigma_{2}^{z},

and therefore belongs to this category with
a_{1}=b
,
a_{2}=c
and
\eta_{11}=\eta_{21}=\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{12}=-\eta_{22}=-\eta_{2}
.

The time evolution operator of a time dependent Hamiltonian is given by the Dyson series or equivalently by the Magnus expansion:

\displaystyle U(t)=\exp{\big\{\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{1}(t)+\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{2}(t)+\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{3}(t)+...\big\}},

(127)

where (in general for
t_{0}\neq 0
)

\displaystyle\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{1}(t,t_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt_{1}H(t_{1})

\displaystyle\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{2}(t,t_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt_{1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}dt_{2}[H(t_{1}),H(t_{2})]

(128)

\displaystyle\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{3}(t,t_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{6}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt_{1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}dt_{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t2}dt_{3}\Big([H(t_{1}),[H(t_{2}),H(t_{3})]]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle[H(t_{3}),[H(t_{2}),H(t_{1})]]\Big),

and so on. In our case,
\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{k}
terms for
k>2
are zero because
[H(s),[H(s^{\prime}),H(s^{\prime\prime})]]=0
. The first term
\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{1}
can be written as

\displaystyle\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{1}(t,t_{0})=-i\sum_{j,m}\eta_{jm}\big[a_{m}G_{jm}(t,t_{0})+a_{m}^{\dagger}G_{jm}^{*}(t,t_{0})\big]\sigma_{j}^{z}

(129)

where

\displaystyle G_{jm}(t,t_{0})=\nu_{m}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt^{\prime}f_{j}(t^{\prime})e^{-i\nu_{m}t^{\prime}}.

(130)

The second term can be calculated to be

\displaystyle\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{2}(t,t_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt_{1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}dt_{2}[H(t_{1}),H(t_{2})]=-\frac{i}{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt_{1}\big[H(t_{1}),\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{1}(t_{1},t_{0})]

(131)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt_{1}\sum_{jm}\sum_{j^{\prime}m^{\prime}}(-i)\eta_{jm}\eta_{j^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\nu_{m}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle[f_{j}(a_{m}e^{-i\nu_{m}t_{1}}+a_{m}^{\dagger}e^{i\nu_{m}t_{1}})\sigma_{j}^{z},(a_{m^{\prime}}G_{j^{\prime}m^{\prime}}+a_{m^{\prime}}^{\dagger}G^{*}_{j^{\prime}m^{\prime}})\sigma_{j^{\prime}}^{z}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt_{1}\sum_{jj^{\prime}}\sum_{m}(-i)\eta_{jm}\eta_{j^{\prime}m}\nu_{m}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\big(f_{j}G^{*}_{j^{\prime}m}e^{-i\nu_{m}t_{1}}[a_{m},a_{m}^{\dagger}]+f_{j}G_{j^{\prime}m}e^{i\nu_{m}t_{1}}[a_{m}^{\dagger},a_{m}]\big)\sigma_{j}^{z}\sigma^{z}_{j^{\prime}},

which, using
[a_{m},a_{m^{\prime}}^{\dagger}]=\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}
and
(\sigma_{j}^{z})^{2}=\mathds{1}
becomes

\displaystyle\Omega^{\textrm{M}}_{2}(t,t_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\varphi(t,t_{0})\sigma_{1}^{z}\sigma_{2}^{z}+K(t,t_{0})\mathds{1},

(132)

where the phase
\varphi
is a time dependent function given by

\displaystyle\varphi(t,t_{0})=\sum_{m}\Im{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt^{\prime}\eta_{1m}\eta_{2m}\nu_{m}\big\{f_{1}(t^{\prime})G_{2m}(t^{\prime},t_{0})+f_{2}(t^{\prime})G_{1m}(t^{\prime},t_{0})\big\}e^{i\nu_{m}t^{\prime}}}.

(133)

where
\Im
indicates the imaginary part. We have ignored the term
K(t)
, as it will only contribute with a global phase. Finally, we can easily check that the time evolution operator can be written as

U(t,t_{0})=U_{s}(t,t_{0})U_{c}(t,t_{0})

(134)

where

U_{s}(t,t_{0})=\exp\bigg\{-\!i\sum_{j,m}\eta_{jm}\left[a_{m}G_{jm}(t,t_{0})+a_{m}^{\dagger}G_{jm}^{*}(t,t_{0})\right]\sigma_{j}^{z}\bigg\},

(135)

and

U_{c}(t,t_{0})=\exp\left[i\varphi(t,t_{0})\sigma_{1}^{z}\sigma_{2}^{z}\right].

(136)

Appendix 7.E
Properties of the
G_{jm}(t)
and
\varphi(t)
functions

Figure 30:
Modulation function
f(x)
function that corresponds to the first two blocks of the AXY-4 pulse sequence. Time has been normalised by the characteristic time of the sequence
\tau
(
x=t/\tau
), as well as
\tilde{\tau}_{a}=\tau_{a}/\tau
and
\tilde{\tau}=\tau_{b}/\tau
.

Searching for all different sequences that fulfil the conditions
G_{jm}(T_{\textrm{G}})=0
and
\varphi(T_{\textrm{G}})\neq 0
gets easy if we identify which are the indispensable variables that define the problem. The sequence function
f_{1}(t)=f_{2}(t)=f(t)
is completely defined by the four parameters
\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau
, and
n_{\textrm{B}}
, for the case of an AXY-
n_{\textrm{B}}
sequence. The duration of the sequence is of course only determined by two of them:
\tau
and
n_{\textrm{B}}
(
T_{\textrm{G}}=n_{\textrm{B}}\tau
). It is useful to rescale the domain on the
f
function using
\tau
, the characteristic time of the sequence, as
t=x\tau
. Then, the property
f(t+\tau)=-f(t)
becomes
f(x+1)=-f(x)
, and also
\tau_{a},\tau_{b}
may be redefined as
\tilde{\tau}_{a}=\tau_{a}/\tau
and
\tilde{\tau}_{b}=\tau_{b}/\tau
. The AXY-4 sequence with this time rescaling is shown in Fig.
30
. With this change of variable, the
G_{jm}
functions at a time
T_{\textrm{G}}
read

G_{jm}(T_{\textrm{G}})=\nu_{m}\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{G}}}\!dt\ f(t)\ e^{-i\nu_{m}t}=\nu_{m}\tau\int_{0}^{M}\!dx\ f(x)\ e^{-i\nu_{m}\tau x}.

(137)

Now, if we relate
\tau
and
\nu
as

\nu\tau=2\pi r\ \ \mbox{with}\ \ r\in\mathbb{N},

(138)

these functions become independent of the frequency
\nu
,

\displaystyle G_{j1}(T_{\textrm{G}})=\nu_{1}\tau\int_{0}^{M}\!dx\ f(x)\ e^{-i2\pi rx}=2\pi r\int_{0}^{M}\!dx\ f(x)\ e^{-i2\pi rx}

(139)

\displaystyle G_{j2}(T_{\textrm{G}})=\nu_{2}\tau\int_{0}^{M}\!dx\ f(x)\ e^{-i2\pi\sqrt{3}rx}=2\pi\sqrt{3}r\int_{0}^{M}\!dx\ f(x)\ e^{-i2\pi\sqrt{3}rx}.

(140)

This simplification works also for the
\varphi(t)
function, that can be redefined in terms of
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(t)
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(t)
as

\displaystyle\varphi(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{1}^{2}\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(t)-\eta_{2}^{2}\tilde{\varphi}_{2}(t)=\eta_{1}^{2}(\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(t)-\frac{1}{3\!\sqrt{3}}\tilde{\varphi}_{2}(t))=\eta_{1}^{2}\tilde{\varphi}(t),

(141)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(T_{\textrm{G}})=(2\pi r)^{2}\ \Im{\int_{0}^{M}\!dx\int_{0}^{x}\!dy\ [f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)+f_{2}(x)f_{1}(y)]\ e^{i2\pi r(x-y)}}

(142)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{2}(T_{\textrm{G}})=(2\pi\sqrt{3}r)^{2}\ \Im{\int_{0}^{M}\!dx\int_{0}^{x}\!dy\ [f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)+f_{2}(x)f_{1}(y)]\ e^{i2\pi\sqrt{3}r(x-y)}},

(143)

or

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}(T_{\textrm{G}})=(2\pi r)^{2}\ \Im{\int_{0}^{M}\!dx\int_{0}^{x}\!dy}[f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)+f_{2}(x)f_{1}(y)]

(144)

\displaystyle\times(e^{i2\pi r(x-y)}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}e^{i2\pi\sqrt{3}r(x-y)}).

(145)

Now, it is clear that the functions
\tilde{G}_{j1}(T_{\textrm{G}})
,
\tilde{G}_{j2}(T_{\textrm{G}})
, and
\tilde{\varphi}(T_{\textrm{G}})
, only depend on
\tilde{\tau}_{a}
,
\tilde{\tau}_{b}
,
n_{\textrm{B}}
and
r
. Therefore, the functions plotted in Fig. (
7
) (corresponding to cases
n_{\textrm{B}}=4
,
r=1,2,3
), do not depend on the frequencies
\nu_{m}
, but only on
\tilde{\tau}_{a}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{b}
.

Appendix 7.F
Pulse propagator

Our strategy to produce fast
\pi
-pulses on a certain ion qubit and, at the same time, eliminate undesired effects on the off-resonant qubit consists in appropriately choosing the Rabi frequency of the driving. When a
\pi
-pulse is applied, say on the first qubit, we have the following Hamiltonian

H=\frac{\omega_{1}}{2}\sigma_{1}^{z}+\frac{\omega_{2}}{2}\sigma_{2}^{z}+\Omega_{1}\cos(\omega_{1}t-\phi)(\sigma_{1}^{x}+\sigma_{2}^{x}).

(146)

In a rotating frame with respect to
\frac{\omega_{1}}{2}\sigma_{1}^{z}+\frac{\omega_{2}}{2}\sigma_{2}^{z}
and after eliminating fast rotating terms, the above Hamiltonian reads

\displaystyle H=\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2}\sigma_{1}^{\phi}+\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2}[\sigma_{2}^{+}e^{i\phi}e^{i\delta_{2}t}+\textrm{H.c.}],

(147)

where
\sigma_{1}^{\phi}=\sigma_{1}^{+}e^{i\phi}+\sigma_{1}^{-}e^{-i\phi}
and
\delta_{2}=\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}
. At this level one can argue that, only when the Rabi frequency is small, the second term on the right-hand side of the above equation is negligible. This unavoidably limits the value of
\Omega_{1}
and, consequently, how fast our decoupling pulses can be. In this respect, note that for a value of
\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}\approx(2\pi)\times 45
MHz, which is even larger than the ones used in section
4
, we find that
\Omega_{1}
should be significantly smaller than
(2\pi)\times 1
MHz if we want to eliminate the crosstalk between ions during 20
\pi
-pulses, see Fig.
31
.

To eliminate this restriction, we can use the following expression

\displaystyle U_{[t:t_{0}]}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\hat{T}e^{-i\int_{t_{0}}^{t}H(s)ds}=U_{0}\tilde{U}_{[t:t_{0}]}

(148)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle e^{-iH_{\delta}(t-t_{0})}\hat{T}e^{-i\int_{t_{0}}^{t}U^{{\dagger}}_{0}(H(s)-H_{\delta})U_{0}ds},

where
\hat{T}
is the time ordering operator,
H_{\delta}=-(\delta_{2}/2)\sigma_{2}^{z}
, and find the time evolution operator for Eq. (
147
) in a generic time interval
(t,t_{0})
. This is

U_{[t:t_{0}]}=e^{-i\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2}\sigma_{1}^{\phi}(t-t_{0})}e^{i\frac{\delta_{2}}{2}\sigma_{2}^{z}(t-t_{0})}e^{-i\gamma(t-t_{0})\hat{n}_{0}\cdot\vec{\sigma}_{2}},

(149)

where
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\Omega_{1}^{2}+\delta_{2}^{2}}
and

\hat{n}_{0}=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\Big[\Omega\cos{(\phi-\varphi_{0})},-\Omega\sin{(\phi+\varphi_{0})},\delta\Big],

(150)

with
\varphi_{0}=\delta t_{0}/2
.

Figure 31:
Fidelity after 20
\pi
-pulses between the propagators associated to the Hamiltonians
H=\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2}\sigma_{1}^{\phi}+\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2}[\sigma_{2}^{+}e^{i\phi}e^{i\delta_{2}t}+{\textrm{H}.c.}]
and
H=\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2}\sigma_{1}^{\phi}
as a function of the Rabi frequency
\Omega
. We can observe how the fidelity decays because of the fail of the RWA. For this plot we have taken
\delta_{2}\approx(2\pi)\times 45
MHz, a value that is even larger than the ones we use in section
4.3
.

Note that the first and the second terms in Eq. (
147
) commute, which allows to apply the relation (
148
) only to the part
\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2}[\sigma_{2}^{+}e^{i\phi}e^{i\delta_{2}t}+\textrm{H.c.}]
. Finally, for the sake of realising a
\pi
-pulse we will set
(t-t_{0})=t^{(1)}_{\pi}\equiv\frac{\pi}{\Omega_{1}}
which gives rise to

\displaystyle U_{t_{\pi}}^{(1)}=e^{-i(\Omega_{1}/2)\sigma_{1}^{\phi}t_{\pi}}e^{i(\delta_{2}/2)\sigma_{2}^{z}t_{\pi}}e^{-i\gamma t_{\pi}(\hat{n}_{0}\cdot\vec{\sigma}_{2})}.

(151)

In the same manner, for a
\pi
-pulse (with
t^{(2)}_{\pi}\equiv\frac{\pi}{\Omega_{2}}
) in resonance with the second ion we would have

\displaystyle U_{t_{\pi}}^{(2)}=e^{-i(\Omega_{2}/2)\sigma_{2}^{\phi}t_{\pi}}e^{i(\delta_{1}/2)\sigma_{1}^{z}t_{\pi}}e^{-i\gamma t_{\pi}(\hat{n}_{0}\cdot\vec{\sigma}_{1})}.

(152)

Equations (
151
) and (
152
) contain the terms corresponding to the
\pi
-pulses in which we are interested (
\exp{[-i\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2}\sigma_{1}^{\phi}t_{\pi}]}
and
\exp{[-i\frac{\Omega_{2}}{2}\sigma_{2}^{\phi}t_{\pi}]}
) plus the crosstalk contributions we want to get rid off. To eliminate terms
\exp{[-i\gamma t_{\pi}(\hat{n}_{0}\cdot\vec{\sigma}_{2})]}
and
\exp{[-i\gamma t_{\pi}(\hat{n}_{0}\cdot\vec{\sigma}_{1})]}
, we will adjust the Rabi frequencies
\Omega_{1,2}
such that

\gamma t_{\pi}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\Omega_{1,2})^{2}+(\delta_{2,1})^{2}}\frac{\pi}{\Omega_{1,2}}=\pi\times k,\mbox{with}\ k\in\mathbb{Z}.

(153)

In this case we have that
\exp{[-i\gamma t_{\pi}(\hat{n}_{0}\cdot\vec{\sigma}_{2})]}=\exp{[-i\gamma t_{\pi}(\hat{n}_{0}\cdot\vec{\sigma}_{1})}]]=\pm\mathds{1}
, i.e. the unwanted terms contribute as a global phase. Hence, only pure dephasing terms remain in both pulses,
\exp{[i\frac{\delta_{2}}{2}\sigma_{2}^{z}t_{\pi}]}
and
\exp{[i\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}\sigma_{1}^{z}t_{\pi}]}
, which will be cancelled by our tailored MW sequences as explained in section
4.3
.

Appendix 7.G
Heating rates estimation

To estimate the
\Gamma_{b,c}
parameters, we will rely on the data provided in
[
276
]
, as well as on the scaling relations one can extract from
[
287
]
. More specifically we take as reference values (for the center-of-mass mode)
\dot{n}_{\textrm{com}}^{\textrm{ref}}=41
phonons/second for a frequency
\nu^{\textrm{ref}}_{1}/(2\pi)=427
kHz, and (for the breathing mode)
\dot{n}_{\textrm{bre}}^{\textrm{ref}}=7
phonons/second for a frequency
\nu^{\textrm{ref}}_{2}/(2\pi)=459
kHz,
[
276
]
. The latter values correspond to a configuration at room temperature (
T^{\textrm{ref}}=300
K) with an ion-electrode distance of
d_{\textrm{i-e}}^{\textrm{ref}}\approx 310\ \mu
m, that would give rise to a magnetic field gradient
g_{B}=23.6
T/m.

Our operating conditions require, for the first studied case, an ion-electrode distance of
d_{\textrm{i-e}}\approx 150\ \mu
m, to generate a magnetic field gradient of
g_{B}=150
T/m where
\nu_{1}=\nu
and
\nu_{2}=\sqrt{3}\nu
with
\nu/(2\pi)=150
kHz, while we will consider low temperatures of
T=50
K. In this situation one can derive new values for
\dot{n}_{\textrm{com}}
and
\dot{n}_{\textrm{bre}}
using scaling relations
[
287
]
which in our case are

\displaystyle\dot{n}_{\textrm{com}}\approx\dot{n}_{\textrm{com}}^{\textrm{ref}}\ \bigg(\frac{\nu_{1}^{\textrm{ref}}}{\nu_{1}}\bigg)^{2}\bigg(\frac{d_{\textrm{i-e}}^{\textrm{ref}}}{d_{\textrm{i-e}}}\bigg)^{4}\bigg(\frac{T^{\textrm{ref}}}{T}\bigg)^{-2.13},

(154)

and

\displaystyle\dot{n}_{\textrm{bre}}\approx\dot{n}_{\textrm{bre}}^{\textrm{ref}}\ \bigg(\frac{\nu_{2}^{\textrm{ref}}}{\nu_{2}}\bigg)^{2}\bigg(\frac{d_{\textrm{i-e}}^{\textrm{ref}}}{d_{\textrm{i-e}}}\bigg)^{4}\bigg(\frac{T^{\textrm{ref}}}{T}\bigg)^{-2.13}.

(155)

Then, one can use that, when close to the motional ground state, we have
[
287
]

\dot{n}_{\textrm{com,bre}}=\Gamma_{b,c}\ \bar{N}_{b,c},

(156)

that together with the definition of
\bar{N}_{b,c}\equiv N^{\textrm{thermal}}_{b,c}=1/(e^{\hbar\nu_{1,2}/k_{\textrm{B}}T}-1)
, allows us to obtain the values for
\Gamma_{b,c}
.

In the second studied case, a magnetic field gradient of
g_{B}=300\frac{\textrm{T}}{\textrm{m}}
would require to locate the ions closer to the electrodes, which would induce more heating. We estimate a distance according to the relation
d_{\textrm{i-e}}=\sqrt{\frac{150}{300}}\ 150\ \mu
m
\approx 106\ \mu
m that assumes a dependence
g_{B}\sim\frac{1}{d_{\textrm{i-e}}^{2}}
. This new distance can be used in Eqs. (
154
) and (
155
) to derive new values for the heating rates
\Gamma_{b,c}
.

Further Considerations on the Selective Interactions of the QRM

Appendix 8.A
Dyson series of the Rabi-Stark Hamiltonian

The Rabi-Stark Hamiltonian as written in Eq. (
40
) is

H=\frac{\omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{z}+\omega a^{\dagger}a+\gamma a^{\dagger}a\sigma_{z}+g(\sigma_{+}+\sigma_{-})(a+a^{\dagger})

(157)

where
\sigma_{z},\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-}
are operators of the two-level system and
a^{\dagger}
and
a
are infinite dimensional creation and annihilation operators of the bosonic field. Using the ket-bra notation, the two-level matrices are
\sigma_{+}=|e\rangle\langle g|
,
\sigma_{-}=|g\rangle\langle e|
and
\sigma_{z}=|e\rangle\langle e|-|g\rangle\langle g|
where
|e\rangle
and
|g\rangle
the excited and ground states of the two level system, respectively. On the other hand, the bosonic operators can be written as

\displaystyle a^{\dagger}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{n+1}|n+1\rangle\langle n|

(158)

\displaystyle a=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{n+1}|n\rangle\langle n+1|

(159)

where
|n\rangle
is the
n
-th Fock state. With this notation, the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
157
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle H=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\omega_{n}^{e}|e\rangle\langle e|\otimes|n\rangle\langle n|+\omega^{g}_{n}|g\rangle\langle g|\otimes|n\rangle\langle n|

\displaystyle+\Omega_{n}(|e\rangle\langle g|+\textrm{H.c.})\otimes(|n+1\rangle\langle n|+\textrm{H.c.})

(160)

where
\omega_{n}^{e}=(\omega+\gamma)n+\omega_{0}/2
,
\omega_{n}^{g}=(\omega-\gamma)n-\omega_{0}/2
and
\Omega_{n}=g\sqrt{n+1}
. We can move to an interaction picture with respect the diagonal part of Eq. (
8.A
), and the non-diagonal elements will rotate as

\displaystyle|e\rangle\langle g|\otimes|n+1\rangle\langle n|

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle|e\rangle\langle g|\otimes|n+1\rangle\langle n|e^{i(\omega^{e}_{n+1}-\omega_{n}^{g})t}

(161)

\displaystyle|g\rangle\langle e|\otimes|n+1\rangle\langle n|

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle|g\rangle\langle e|\otimes|n+1\rangle\langle n|e^{-i(\omega^{e}_{n}-\omega_{n+1}^{g})t}

(162)

\displaystyle|e\rangle\langle g|\otimes|n\rangle\langle n+1|

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle|e\rangle\langle g|\otimes|n\rangle\langle n+1|e^{i(\omega^{e}_{n}-\omega_{n+1}^{g})t}

(163)

\displaystyle|g\rangle\langle e|\otimes|n\rangle\langle n+1|

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle|g\rangle\langle e|\otimes|n\rangle\langle n+1|e^{-i(\omega^{e}_{n+1}-\omega_{n}^{g})t}

(164)

where
\delta^{+}_{n}=\omega_{n+1}^{e}-\omega_{n}^{g}=\omega+[\omega_{0}+\gamma(2n+1)]
and
\delta^{-}_{n}=\omega_{n+1}^{g}-\omega_{n}^{e}=\omega-[\omega_{0}+\gamma(2n+1)]
. The Hamiltonian in the interaction picture can be then rewritten as

H_{I}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(\sigma_{+}e^{i\delta^{+}_{n}t}+\sigma_{-}e^{i\delta^{-}_{n}t})\otimes|n+1\rangle\langle n|+\textrm{H.c.}

(165)

which corresponds to Eq. (
41
).

8.A.1
Second-order Hamiltonian

The second order Hamiltonian that corresponds to Eq. (
165
) is given by
[
153
]

H^{(2)}(t)=-i\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}H_{I}(t)H_{I}(t^{\prime}).

(166)

We can write
H_{I}(t)
as

H_{I}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}\Big(S_{n}(t)|n+1\rangle\langle n|+S^{\dagger}_{n}(t)|n\rangle\langle n+1|\Big),

(167)

and, then,

\displaystyle H^{(2)}(t)=-i\sum_{n,n^{\prime}}\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n^{\prime}}\Big(S_{n}(t)|n+1\rangle\langle n|+S^{\dagger}_{n}(t)|n\rangle\langle n+1|\Big)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\Big(S_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})|n^{\prime}+1\rangle\langle n^{\prime}|+S^{\dagger}_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})|n^{\prime}\rangle\langle n^{\prime}+1|\Big),

(168)

which gives
H^{(2)}=H_{A}^{(2)}+H_{B}^{(2)}
, where

\displaystyle H^{(2)}_{A}(t)=-i\sum_{n}\Omega_{n}^{2}\Big(S_{n}(t)\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}S^{\dagger}_{n}(t^{\prime})\Big)|n+1\rangle\langle n+1|

\displaystyle+\Omega^{2}_{n}\Big(S^{\dagger}_{n}(t)\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}S_{n}(t^{\prime})\Big)|n\rangle\langle n|

(169)

gives diagonal elements and

\displaystyle H^{(2)}_{B}(t)=-i\sum_{n}\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n+1}\Big(S_{n+1}(t)\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}S_{n}(t^{\prime})\Big)|n+2\rangle\langle n|

\displaystyle+\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n+1}\Big(S^{\dagger}_{n}(t)\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}S^{\dagger}_{n+1}(t^{\prime})\Big)|n\rangle\langle n+2|

(170)

is related with two-photon processes. Calculating the two-level operators we obtain

\displaystyle S_{n}(t)\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}S^{\dagger}_{n}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\delta^{+}_{n}}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}+\frac{i}{\delta^{-}_{n}}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}

(171)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\delta^{+}_{n}}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}e^{i\delta^{+}_{n}t}-\frac{i}{\delta^{-}_{n}}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}e^{i\delta^{-}_{n}t}

\displaystyle S^{\dagger}_{n}(t)\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}S_{n}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{\delta^{+}_{n}}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}-\frac{i}{\delta^{-}_{n}}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}

(172)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\delta^{+}_{n}}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}e^{-i\delta^{+}_{n}t}+\frac{i}{\delta^{-}_{n}}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}e^{-i\delta^{-}_{n}t}

\displaystyle S_{n+1}(t)\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}S_{n}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{\delta^{-}_{n}}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}(e^{i(\delta^{+}_{n+1}+\delta^{-}_{n})t}-e^{i\delta^{+}_{n+1}t})

(173)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\delta^{+}_{n}}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}(e^{i(\delta^{-}_{n+1}+\delta^{+}_{n})t}-e^{i\delta^{-}_{n+1}t})

\displaystyle S^{\dagger}_{n}(t)\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}S^{\dagger}_{n+1}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\delta^{-}_{n+1}}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}(e^{-i(\delta^{+}_{n}+\delta^{-}_{n+1})t}-e^{-i\delta^{+}_{n}t})

(174)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\delta^{+}_{n+1}}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}(e^{-i(\delta^{-}_{n}+\delta^{+}_{n+1})t}-e^{i\delta^{-}_{n}t}).

We can ignore the terms oscillating with
\pm\delta^{\pm}_{n}
, as these frequencies correspond to resonances of the first-order Hamiltonian and one-photon processes. Keeping the other terms we have that

\displaystyle H^{(2)}_{A}\approx\sum_{n}\Omega_{n}^{2}\Big(\frac{\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}}{\delta_{n}^{+}}+\frac{\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}}{\delta_{n}^{-}}\Big)|n+1\rangle\langle n+1|-\Omega^{2}_{n}\Big(\frac{\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}}{\delta^{+}_{n}}+\frac{\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}}{\delta^{-}_{n}}\Big)|n\rangle\langle n|

(175)

and

\displaystyle H^{(2)}_{B}(t)\approx\sum_{n}-\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n+1}\Big(\frac{\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}}{\delta^{-}_{n}}e^{i(\delta^{+}_{n+1}+\delta^{-}_{n})t}+\frac{\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}}{\delta^{+}_{n}}e^{i(\delta^{-}_{n+1}+\delta^{+}_{n})t}\Big)|n+2\rangle\langle n|

\displaystyle+\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n+1}\Big(\frac{\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}}{\delta^{-}_{n+1}}e^{-i(\delta^{+}_{n}+\delta^{-}_{n+1})t}+\frac{\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}}{\delta^{+}_{n+1}}e^{-i(\delta^{-}_{n}+\delta^{+}_{n+1})t}\Big)|n\rangle\langle n+2|.

(176)

The two-photon transition terms in Eq. (
8.A.1
) oscillate with frequencies
\delta^{+}_{n}+\delta^{-}_{n+1}=2\omega-2\gamma
and
\delta^{+}_{n+1}+\delta^{-}_{n}=2\omega+2\gamma
, which are zero only in the points of the spectral collapse. Thus, we do not expect to see two-photon transitions in the regime where the Hamiltonian is bounded from below. The terms in Eq. (
175
) will induce an additional Stark shift that can induce a shift in the resonance conditions of the higher order processes, as we will see later. The Hamiltonian can be simplified to

H^{(2)}_{A}\approx\sum_{n}\Bigg\{\Big(\frac{\Omega_{n-1}^{2}}{\delta_{n-1}^{+}}-\frac{\Omega_{n}^{2}}{\delta_{n}^{-}}\Big)\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}+\Big(\frac{\Omega_{n-1}^{2}}{\delta_{n-1}^{-}}-\frac{\Omega_{n}^{2}}{\delta_{n}^{+}}\Big)\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}\Bigg\}|n\rangle\langle n|.

(177)

8.A.2
Third-order Hamiltonian

The third order Hamiltonian is calculated by

H^{(3)}(t)=(-i)^{2}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}dt^{\prime\prime}H_{I}(t)H_{I}(t^{\prime})H(t^{\prime\prime}).

(178)

Following the same notation of the previous section, the third order Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H^{(3)}(t)=-\sum_{n,n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}}\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n^{\prime}}\Omega_{n^{\prime\prime}}\Big(S_{n}(t)|n+1\rangle\langle n|+\textrm{H.c.}\Big)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\Big(S_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})|n^{\prime}+1\rangle\langle n^{\prime}|+\textrm{H.c.}\Big)\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}dt^{\prime\prime}\Big(S_{n^{\prime\prime}}(t^{\prime\prime})|n^{\prime\prime}+1\rangle\langle n^{\prime\prime}|+\textrm{H.c.}\Big).

(179)

If we focus on the three-photon resonances, the following Hamiltonian contains them

\displaystyle H_{A}^{(3)}(t)=-\sum_{n}\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n+1}\Omega_{n+2}S_{n+2}(t)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}S_{n+1}(t^{\prime})\Big(\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}dt^{\prime\prime}S_{n}(t^{\prime\prime})\Big)|n+3\rangle\langle n|+\textrm{H.c}.

(180)

The contribution of the two-level operators can be easily calculated by noticing that from

\displaystyle S_{n+2}(t)S_{n+1}(t^{\prime})S_{n}(t^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\sigma_{+}e^{i\delta^{+}_{n+2}t}+\sigma_{-}e^{i\delta^{-}_{n+2}t})

(181)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle(\sigma_{+}e^{i\delta^{+}_{n+1}t^{\prime}}+\sigma_{-}e^{i\delta^{-}_{n+1}t^{\prime}})(\sigma_{+}e^{i\delta^{+}_{n}t^{\prime\prime}}+\sigma_{-}e^{i\delta^{-}_{n}t^{\prime\prime}}),

only the following two terms are not zero (notice that
\sigma_{\pm}^{2}=0
)

S_{n+2}(t)S_{n+1}(t^{\prime})S_{n}(t^{\prime\prime})=\sigma_{+}e^{i\delta^{+}_{n+2}t}e^{i\delta^{-}_{n+1}t^{\prime}}e^{i\delta^{+}_{n}t^{\prime\prime}}+\sigma_{-}e^{i\delta^{-}_{n+2}t}e^{i\delta^{+}_{n+1}t^{\prime}}e^{i\delta^{-}_{n}t^{\prime\prime}}.

(182)

After calculating the integral we obtain that the Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H_{A}^{(3)}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n+1}\Omega_{n+2}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\delta_{n}^{+}(\delta_{n+1}^{-}+\delta_{n}^{+})}\Big(e^{i\delta^{(3)}_{+n}t}-e^{i\delta_{n+2}^{+}t}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\delta^{+}_{n}\delta_{n}^{-}}\Big(e^{i2(\omega+\gamma)t}-e^{i\delta_{n+2}^{+}t}\Big)\Bigg\}\sigma_{+}|n+3\rangle\langle n|+\textrm{H.c}

\displaystyle+\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n+1}\Omega_{n+2}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\delta_{n}^{-}(\delta_{n+1}^{+}+\delta_{n}^{-})}\Big(e^{i\delta^{(3)}_{-n}t}-e^{i\delta_{n+2}^{-}t}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\delta^{-}_{n}\delta_{n}^{+}}\Big(e^{i2(\omega-\gamma)t}-e^{i\delta_{n+2}^{-}t}\Big)\Bigg\}\sigma_{-}|n\rangle\langle n+3|+\textrm{H.c}

(183)

where
\delta^{(3)}_{+n}=\delta_{n+2}^{+}+\delta_{n+1}^{-}+\delta_{n}^{+}=2\omega+\delta^{+}_{n+1}
and
\delta^{(3)}_{-n}=\delta_{n+2}^{-}+\delta_{n+1}^{+}+\delta_{n}^{-}=2\omega+\delta^{-}_{n+1}
. Ignoring the resonances
\delta^{+}_{n+2}
or
\delta^{-}_{n+2}
that correspond to the first-order processes, and
2(\omega\pm\gamma)
which is only zero at the point of the spectral collapse we are leaved with

\displaystyle H_{A}^{(3)}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n+1}\Omega_{n+2}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{2\delta_{n}^{+}(\omega-\gamma)}\sigma_{+}e^{i\delta^{(3)}_{n+}t}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\delta_{n}^{-}(\omega+\gamma)}\sigma_{-}e^{i\delta^{(3)}_{n-}t}\Bigg\}|n+3\rangle\langle n|+\textrm{H.c},

(184)

where the 3-photon JC and anti-JC resonances are easily identified as
\delta_{n+}^{(3)}=0
and
\delta_{n-}^{(3)}=0
respectively. In a simplified way, Eq. (
8.A.2
) is rewritten as

H_{A}^{(3)}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\Omega^{(3)}_{n+}\sigma_{+}e^{i\delta^{(3)}_{n+}t}+\Omega_{n-}^{(3)}\sigma_{-}e^{i\delta^{(3)}_{n-}t})|n+3\rangle\langle n|+\textrm{H.c},

(185)

where
\Omega^{(3)}_{n+}=g^{3}\sqrt{(n+3)!/n!}/2\delta^{+}_{n}(\omega-\gamma)
and
\Omega^{(3)}_{n-}=g^{3}\sqrt{(n+3)!/n!}/2\delta^{-}_{n}(\omega+\gamma)
, as shown in section
6.2
.

Appendix 8.B
Derivation of the Rabi-Stark Hamiltonian in trapped ions

In this section we will explain in detail how to go from Eq. (
48
) to Eq. (
49
) in the main text. Equation (
48
) reads

H_{A}(t)=-i\frac{\eta\Omega_{r}}{2}a\sigma_{+}e^{-i\delta_{r}t}-i\frac{\eta\Omega_{b}}{2}a^{\dagger}\sigma_{+}e^{-i\delta_{b}t}+e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\hat{g}_{\textrm{S}}\sigma_{+}+\textrm{H.c.}

(186)

At this point the vibrational RWA has been applied, however for a precise understanding of the effective dynamics we need to consider the following additional terms that have been neglected, so that the total Hamiltonian is
H=H_{A}+H_{B}
, where

\displaystyle H_{B}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\Omega_{r}}{2}\sigma_{+}e^{-i(-\nu+\delta_{r})t}-\frac{\Omega_{b}}{2}\sigma_{+}e^{-i(\nu+\delta_{b})t}

(187)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle i\eta e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}\sigma_{+}(ae^{-i\nu t}+a^{\dagger}e^{i\nu t})+\textrm{H.c.}

The first two terms are the off-resonant carrier interactions of the red and blue drivings which are usually neglected given that
\Omega_{r,b}\ll\nu
. The last term represents the coupling to the motional mode of the carrier driving. The latter does not commute with the first and second terms, and moreover, they both oscillate at similar frequencies (as
\delta_{r},\delta_{b}\ll\nu
). The consequence is that, at second order, these terms produce interactions that cannot be neglected as we will see in the following. The second order effective Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H^{(2)}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\int_{0}^{t}H(t)H(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}

(188)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\int_{0}^{t}\Big(H_{A}(t)+H_{B}(t)\Big)\Big(H_{A}(t^{\prime})+H_{B}(t^{\prime})\Big)dt^{\prime}.

We are only interested in terms arising from
\int_{0}^{t}H_{B}(t)H_{B}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}
whose oscillating frequency is
\delta_{r}
or
\delta_{b}
. These are

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{r}}{2}\sigma_{+}e^{-i(-\nu+\delta_{r})t}\int_{0}^{t}\!\!dt^{\prime}i\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{-}ae^{-i\nu t^{\prime}}=-\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{r}}{4\nu}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{-i\delta_{r}t}a

(189)

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{b}}{2}\sigma_{+}e^{-i(\nu+\delta_{b})t}\int_{0}^{t}\!\!dt^{\prime}i\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{-}a^{\dagger}e^{i\nu t^{\prime}}=\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{b}}{4\nu}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{-i\delta_{b}t}a^{\dagger}

(190)

\displaystyle-i\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{+}a^{\dagger}e^{i\nu t}\int_{0}^{t}\!\!dt^{\prime}\frac{\Omega_{r}}{2}\sigma_{-}e^{i(-\nu+\delta_{r})t^{\prime}}=\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{r}}{4(\nu-\delta_{r})}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{i\delta_{r}t}a^{\dagger}

(191)

\displaystyle-i\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{+}ae^{-i\nu t}\int_{0}^{t}\!\!dt^{\prime}\frac{\Omega_{b}}{2}\sigma_{-}e^{i(\nu+\delta_{b})t^{\prime}}=-\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{b}}{4(\nu+\delta_{b})}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{i\delta_{b}t}a

(192)

\displaystyle-\frac{\Omega_{r}}{2}\sigma_{-}e^{i(-\nu+\delta_{r})t}\int_{0}^{t}\!\!dt^{\prime}i\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{+}a^{\dagger}e^{i\nu t^{\prime}}=-\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{r}}{4\nu}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{i\delta_{r}t}a^{\dagger}

(193)

\displaystyle-\frac{\Omega_{b}}{2}\sigma_{-}e^{i(\nu+\delta_{b})t}\int_{0}^{t}\!\!dt^{\prime}i\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{+}ae^{-i\nu t^{\prime}}=\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{b}}{4\nu}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{i\delta_{b}t}a

(194)

\displaystyle i\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{-}ae^{-i\nu t}\int_{0}^{t}\!\!dt^{\prime}\frac{\Omega_{r}}{2}\sigma_{+}e^{-i(-\nu+\delta_{r})t^{\prime}}=\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{r}}{4(\nu-\delta_{r})}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{-i\delta_{r}t}a

(195)

\displaystyle i\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{-}a^{\dagger}e^{i\nu t}\int_{0}^{t}\!\!dt^{\prime}\frac{\Omega_{b}}{2}\sigma_{+}e^{-i(\nu+\delta_{b})t^{\prime}}=-\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{b}}{4(\nu+\delta_{b})}\sigma_{-}\sigma_{+}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{-i\delta_{b}t}a^{\dagger}.

(196)

If we assume that
1/(\nu\pm\delta_{j})\sim 1/\nu
and reorganise all the terms we get that the second-order effective Hamiltonian is

H_{B}^{(2)}(t)\approx\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{r}}{4\nu}(ie^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{-i\delta_{r}t}a+\textrm{H.c.})\sigma_{z}-\eta\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{b}}{4\nu}(ie^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}e^{-i\delta_{b}t}a^{\dagger}+\textrm{H.c.})\sigma_{z}

(197)

which can be incorporated to the first-order Hamiltonian in Eq. (
186
) giving

\displaystyle H_{\textrm{ eff}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i(2g^{(1)}_{r}\sigma_{+}-g^{(2)}_{r}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{z})ae^{-i\delta_{r}t}-i(2g_{b}^{(1)}\sigma_{+}+g_{b}^{(2)}e^{-i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}\sigma_{z})a^{\dagger}e^{-i\delta_{b}t}

(198)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{+}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}-\eta^{2}\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}a^{\dagger}a\sigma_{+}e^{i\phi_{\textrm{S}}}+\textrm{H.c.},

where
g_{r,b}^{(1)}=\eta\Omega_{r,b}/4
and
g_{r,b}^{(2)}=\eta\Omega_{\textrm{S}}\Omega_{r,b}/4\nu
and
\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{\textrm{S}}(1-\eta^{2}/2)
. Now, if we assume
\phi_{\textrm{S}}=0
or
\pi
and move to a frame with respect
\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}}{2}\sigma_{x}
, we obtain (below
\Omega\equiv\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}
for clarity)

\displaystyle H_{\textrm{eff}}^{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{0}^{R}}{2}\sigma_{+}\mp\eta^{2}\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}a^{\dagger}a\sigma_{+}-i\Big(g^{(1)}_{r}(\sigma_{x}+i\sigma_{y}e^{-i\Omega t\sigma_{x}})\mp g^{(2)}_{r}\sigma_{z}e^{-i\Omega t\sigma_{x}}\Big)ae^{-i\delta_{r}t}

(199)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle i\Big(g_{b}^{(1)}(\sigma_{x}+i\sigma_{y}e^{-i\Omega t\sigma_{x}})\pm g_{b}^{(2)}\sigma_{z}e^{-i\Omega t\sigma_{x}}\Big)a^{\dagger}e^{-i\delta_{b}t}+\textrm{H.c.},

where
\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}=\pm\Omega_{0}-\omega_{0}^{\textrm{ R}}
for
\phi_{\textrm{S}}=0
and
\phi_{\textrm{S}}=\pi
respectively. Using that

\displaystyle\sigma_{y}e^{-i\Omega t\sigma_{x}}=\cos{(\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t)}\sigma_{y}-\sin{(\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t)}\sigma_{z}=\tilde{\sigma}_{+}e^{i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}+\tilde{\sigma}_{-}e^{-i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}

(200)

\displaystyle\sigma_{z}e^{-i\Omega t\sigma_{x}}=\cos{(\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t)}\sigma_{z}+\sin{(\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t)}\sigma_{y}=-i(\tilde{\sigma}_{+}e^{i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}-\tilde{\sigma}_{-}e^{-i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}),

(201)

where
\tilde{\sigma}_{\pm}=(\sigma_{y}\pm i\sigma_{z})/2
, and that the detunings are chosen to be
\delta_{r}=\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}+\omega^{\textrm{ R}}
and
\delta_{b}=\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}-\omega^{\textrm{ R}}
, Eq. (
198
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle H_{\textrm{eff}}^{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{0}^{\textrm{ R}}}{2}\sigma_{+}\mp\eta^{2}\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}a^{\dagger}a\sigma_{+}

(202)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Big(g^{(1)}_{r}(-i\sigma_{x}+[\tilde{\sigma}_{+}e^{i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}+\textrm{H.c.}])\pm g^{(2)}_{r}(\tilde{\sigma}_{+}e^{i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}-\textrm{H.c.}\Big)ae^{-i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}e^{-i\omega^{\textrm{ R}}t}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Big(g_{b}^{(1)}(-i\sigma_{x}+[\tilde{\sigma}_{+}e^{i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}+\textrm{H.c.}])\mp g_{b}^{(2)}(\tilde{\sigma}_{+}e^{i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}-\textrm{H.c.}\Big)a^{\dagger}e^{-i\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}t}e^{i\omega^{\textrm{ R}}t}

\displaystyle+

H.c.

where all terms rotating with
\pm\Omega_{\textrm{DD}}
or higher can be neglected by the RWA. After the approximation we have

\displaystyle H_{\textrm{eff}}^{I}=\frac{\omega_{0}^{\textrm{ R}}}{2}\sigma_{x}\mp\eta^{2}\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}a^{\dagger}a\sigma_{+}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(g^{(1)}_{r}\pm g^{(2)}_{r})\tilde{\sigma}_{+}ae^{-i\omega^{\textrm{ R}}t}

(203)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(g_{b}^{(1)}\mp g_{b}^{(2)})\tilde{\sigma}_{+}a^{\dagger}e^{i\omega^{\textrm{ R}}t}+\textrm{H.c.}

which in a rotating frame with respect
-\omega^{\textrm{ R}}a^{\dagger}a
is

H_{\textrm{eff}}^{II}=\frac{\omega^{\textrm{ R}}_{0}}{2}\sigma_{x}+\omega^{\textrm{ R}}a^{\dagger}a+g_{\textrm{JC}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{+}a+\tilde{\sigma}_{-}a^{\dagger})+g_{\textrm{aJC}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{+}a^{\dagger}+\tilde{\sigma}_{-}a)\mp\eta^{2}\frac{\Omega_{\textrm{S}}}{2}a^{\dagger}a\sigma_{x}

(204)

where
g_{\textrm{JC}}=\eta\Omega_{r}(1\pm\Omega_{\textrm{S}}/\nu)/4
and
g_{\textrm{aJC}}=\eta\Omega_{b}(1\mp\Omega_{\textrm{S}}/\nu)/4
, depending on the choice of the phase
\phi_{\textrm{S}}=0
or
\phi_{\textrm{S}}=\pi
.

Further Considerations on Boson Sampling with Ultracold Atoms

Appendix 9.A
Pair distribution in the optical lattice

To estimate the rate of two-body collisions in section
9
, we need to know the probability of finding
k
pairs of atoms occupying the same lattice sites, regardless of their spin states. To calculate this probability, we assume that the quantum circuit realises a Haar-random unitary matrix
U
and, importantly, that the system is in a linear superposition of all possible states, where the probabilities of all states are the same. This second assumption, namely the uniform distribution (on average) over all possible bosonic configurations, has been proven for the output modes of the quantum circuit
[
399
]
and, for convenience, it is assumed valid in this work for the intermediate stages of the quantum circuit too. This will be justified by exact numerical simulations in section
9.2
.

We can calculate the probability of finding exactly
k_{2}
pairs by simply counting the number of configurations with
k_{2}
pairs of atoms in the same site, and dividing it by the overall number of possible bosonic configurations.
This latter is given by the multiset coefficient
\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\big)
. To determine the number of configurations containing
k_{2}
pairs, we should first consider that there are
\binom{M/2}{k_{2}}
different ways in which
k_{2}
pairs of atoms can be arranged in
M/2
sites. Since for each site doubly occupied, there are three possible spin configurations,
|2\rangle_{\downarrow}|0\rangle_{\uparrow}
,

|1\rangle_{\downarrow}|1\rangle_{\uparrow}
, and
|0\rangle_{\downarrow}|2\rangle_{\uparrow}
, the previous number of combinations should be multiplied by
3^{k_{2}}
to obtain the total number of configurations. Secondly, we should consider that there are
\binom{M/2-k_{2}}{N-2k_{2}}
combinations in which the remaining
N-2k_{2}
atoms could be arranged in the remaining
M/2-k_{2}
sites. Since for each site singly occupied, there are only two possible spin configurations,
|1\rangle_{\downarrow}|0\rangle_{\uparrow}
and
|0\rangle_{\downarrow}|1\rangle_{\uparrow}
, the previous number of combinations should be multiplied by
2^{N-2k_{2}}
to obtain the total number of configurations in which the
N-2k_{2}
single atoms could be arranged. Thus, the probability of finding
k_{2}
pairs of atoms in distinct sites is:

P_{\text{pair}}(N,M;k_{2})=\frac{3^{k_{2}}\binom{M/2}{k_{2}}2^{N-2k_{2}}\binom{M/2-k_{2}}{N-2k_{2}}}{\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\big)}\Big.

(205)

Equation (
205
) only takes into account particle pairs, forgetting about states that have more than two particles in a site. Those can be taken into account defining a more general expression
P(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},...)
, corresponding to the probability of having
k_{2}
pairs,
k_{3}
trios,
k_{4}
quartets and so on. For simplicity, we will consider the case for only pairs and trios. The number of configurations in which
k_{3}
trios can be placed in
M/2
sites is given by
\binom{M/2}{k_{3}}
. This number has to be multiplied by
4^{k_{3}}
, as
4
different states can represent a trio in a site:
|3\rangle_{\downarrow}|0\rangle_{\uparrow}
,

|2\rangle_{\downarrow}|1\rangle_{\uparrow}
,
|1\rangle_{\downarrow}|2\rangle_{\uparrow}
and
|0\rangle_{\downarrow}|3\rangle_{\uparrow}
. Then, the number of configurations in which
k_{2}
pairs can be placed in the remaining
M/2-k_{3}
sites is given by
\binom{M/2-k_{3}}{k_{2}}
. Finally, the number of configurations in which the remaining
N-2k_{2}-3k_{3}
particles can be placed in
N-2k_{2}-3k_{3}
sites is given by
\binom{M/2-k_{2}-k_{3}}{N-2k_{2}-3k_{3}}
. The probability of having
k_{2}
pairs and
k_{3}
trios is then given by

P(k_{2},k_{3})=4^{k_{3}}\binom{M/2}{k_{3}}3^{k_{2}}\binom{M/2-k_{3}}{k_{2}}2^{N-2k_{2}-3k_{3}}\binom{M/2-k_{2}-k_{3}}{N-2k_{2}-3k_{3}}\Big/\bigg(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\bigg).

(206)

In the limit of large
N
, both Eqs. (
205
) and (
206
) converge to a Poissonian distribution

\lim_{N\to\infty}P_{\text{pair}}(N,M;k)=\frac{\lambda^{k}}{e^{\lambda}k!},

(207)

with average value
\lambda=3/2c
. Here, the constant factor
c
denotes the ratio
c=M/N^{2}
, which in the main text has been simply assumed equal to 1. As an example, the probability associated to the collision-free subspace (the subspace of states where all atoms are at different sites), i.e.
P_{\text{pair}}(N,M;0)
, tends to
1/\exp(3/2c)
for large
N
.

To prove how Eq. (
206
) tends to Eq. (
207
) for large
N
, it is useful to recall that

\bigg(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\bigg)=\frac{M^{N}}{N!}\prod_{a=0}^{N-1}(1+a/M)\approx\exp{(\sum_{a=0}^{N-1}a/M)}\approx\exp{(N^{2}/2M)},

(208)

and

\binom{M}{N}=\frac{M^{N}}{N!}\prod_{a=0}^{N-1}(1-a/M)\approx\exp{(-\sum_{a=0}^{N-1}a/M)}\approx\exp{(-N^{2}/2M)},

(209)

for
M\ll N
. Using these relations, Eq. (
206
) can be rewritten as

P(k_{2},k_{3})\approx\frac{1}{k_{2}!k_{3}!}\bigg(\frac{N^{3}}{2M^{2}}\bigg)^{k_{3}}\bigg(\frac{3N^{2}}{2M}\bigg)^{k_{2}}e^{-3N^{2}/2M}.

(210)

For a
M=cN^{2}
scaling,
N^{3}/2M^{2}
tends to zero, which means that the probability of having trios tends to zero for large
N
. Of course, the same will happen with the probability of having four or more particles in a site. For
k_{3}=0
, the probability of having
k_{2}
pairs can be written as

P_{\textrm{ pair}}(k_{2})=P(k_{2},0)\approx\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\bigg(\frac{3}{2c}\bigg)^{k_{2}}e^{-3/2c},

(211)

which equals Eq. (
207
).

Appendix 9.B
Derivation of average values of
V
and
V^{2}

In the following we present a derivation of the average value of
V
and
V^{2}
evaluated in a uniform state,
|\psi\rangle_{u}={D}^{-1/2}\sum_{d=1}^{D}|d\rangle
, where
D=\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\big)
. It may be useful to recall the form of
V
,

V=\frac{\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}}{4}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\hat{n}_{m}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)+\frac{\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}}{2}\sum_{s=1}^{M/2}\hat{n}_{2s-1}\hat{n}_{2s}.

(212)

Then,
\langle V\rangle_{u}
is

\langle V\rangle_{u}=\frac{\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}}{4}M\langle\hat{n}_{m}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)\rangle_{u}+\frac{\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}}{2}\frac{M}{2}\langle\hat{n}_{2s-1}\hat{n}_{2s}\rangle_{u},

(213)

where we used that, because of the symmetry of the uniform state,
\langle\sum_{m=1}^{M}\hat{A}_{m}\rangle_{u}=M\langle\hat{A}_{m}\rangle_{u}
.
To evaluate
\langle\hat{n}_{m}\rangle_{u}
, we can think on the number of states that can have
k
bosons in mode
m
. This is just equivalent to the number of configurations in which you can put
N-k
particles in
M-1
modes, which is given by
\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M-1}{N-k}\hskip-2.5pt\big)
.
\langle\hat{n}_{m}\rangle_{u}
is then given by
\sum_{k=0}^{N}kp_{k}
, where
p_{k}=\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M-1}{N-k}\hskip-2.5pt\big)/\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\big)
. In the same way,
\langle\hat{n}_{m}^{2}\rangle_{u}
is given by
\sum_{k=0}^{N}k^{2}p_{k}
. We call
p_{k}
, the probability of having
k
particles in mode
m
. To evaluate
\langle\hat{n}_{m}\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}\rangle_{u}
, one needs to take into account the probability of having
k
particles in mode
m
while having
k^{\prime}
particles in mode
m^{\prime}
, which is given by
p_{k,k^{\prime}}=\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M-2}{N-k-k^{\prime}}\hskip-2.5pt\big)/\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\big)
.
\langle\hat{n}_{m}\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}\rangle_{u}
is then given by
\sum_{k=0}^{N}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{N-k}kk^{\prime}p_{k,k^{\prime}}
. Using all this,

\langle V\rangle_{u}=\frac{\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}}{4}M\sum_{k=0}^{N}p_{k}k(k-1)+\frac{\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}}{2}\frac{M}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{N-k}p_{k,k^{\prime}}kk^{\prime}.

(214)

To make the calculation of the summations in Eq. (
214
) easier, we can make use of

\bigg(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N-k}\hskip-2.5pt\bigg)/\bigg(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\bigg)\sim\frac{N^{k}}{(M+N)^{k}}

(215)

when
k\ll N,M

[
399
]
. Then,
p_{k}\approx a_{1}\lambda_{1}^{k}
and
p_{k,k^{\prime}}\approx a_{2}\lambda_{2}^{(k+k^{\prime})}
, where
a_{j}=\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{M-i}{M+N-i}
and
\lambda_{j}=\frac{N}{M+N-j}
. The summations can be then reduced to geometric series, for example,

\sum^{N}_{k=0}k(k-1)p_{k}=a_{1}\lambda_{1}^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial k^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\lambda_{1}^{k}\approx a_{1}\lambda_{1}^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial k^{2}}\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{1}}=\frac{2a_{1}\lambda_{1}^{2}}{(1-\lambda_{1})^{3}}.

(216)

Now, assuming that
M=cN^{2}
, one can calculate the limit at large
N
of
M2a_{1}\lambda_{1}^{2}/(1-\lambda_{1})^{3}
, which gives
2/c
. The rest of the summations are done using Wolfram Mathematica, obtaining
\langle V\rangle_{u}=3\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}/4c
.

To calculate
\langle V^{2}\rangle_{u}
, the same approach is followed. First, we write
V^{2}
,

\displaystyle V^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}^{2}}{16}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{M}\hat{n}_{m}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}(\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}-1)

(217)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}^{2}}{4}\sum_{s=1}^{M/2}\sum_{s^{\prime}=1}^{M/2}\hat{n}_{2s-1}\hat{n}_{2s}\hat{n}_{2s^{\prime}-1}\hat{n}_{2s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{\textrm{ tb}}^{2}}{4}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{s=1}^{M/2}\hat{n}_{m}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)\hat{n}_{2s-1}\hat{n}_{2s}.

The first term can be written as

\displaystyle\langle\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{M}\hat{n}_{m}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}(\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}-1)\rangle_{u}

(218)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M\langle\hat{n}_{m}^{2}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)^{2}\rangle_{u}+M(M-1)\langle\hat{n}_{m}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}(\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}-1)\rangle_{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M\sum_{k=0}^{N}p_{k}k^{2}(k-1)^{2}+M(M-1)\sum_{k=0}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{N-k}p_{k,k^{\prime}}k(k-1)k^{\prime}(k^{\prime}-1).

Similar with the second term

\displaystyle\langle\sum_{s=1}^{M/2}\sum_{s^{\prime}=1}^{M/2}\hat{n}_{2s-1}\hat{n}_{2s}\hat{n}_{2s^{\prime}-1}\hat{n}_{2s^{\prime}}\rangle_{u}

(219)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M}{2}\langle\hat{n}_{2s-1}^{2}\hat{n}_{2s}^{2}\rangle_{u}+\frac{M}{2}(\frac{M}{2}-1)\langle\hat{n}_{2s-1}\hat{n}_{2s}\hat{n}_{2s^{\prime}-1}\hat{n}_{2s^{\prime}}\rangle_{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{N-k}p_{k,k^{\prime}}k^{2}k^{\prime 2}+\frac{M}{2}(\frac{M}{2}-1)\sum_{k=0}^{N}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{N^{\prime}}\sum_{k^{\prime\prime}=0}^{N^{\prime\prime}}\sum_{k^{\prime\prime\prime}=0}^{N^{\prime\prime\prime}}p_{k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime\prime}}kk^{\prime}k^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime\prime\prime},

where
N^{\prime}=N-k
,
N^{\prime\prime}=N-k-k^{\prime}
,
N^{\prime\prime\prime}=N-k-k^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime}
, and
p_{k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime\prime}}=\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M-4}{N-k-k^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime}-k^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-2.5pt\big)/\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\big)
, which can be approximated to
p_{k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime\prime}}\approx a_{4}\lambda_{4}^{(k+k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+k^{\prime\prime\prime})}
. The third term is:

\displaystyle\langle\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{s=1}^{M/2}\hat{n}_{m}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)\hat{n}_{2s-1}\hat{n}_{2s}\rangle_{u}

(220)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M\langle\hat{n}_{m}^{2}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}\rangle_{u}+\frac{M}{2}(M-1)\langle\hat{n}_{m}(\hat{n}_{m}-1)\hat{n}_{m^{\prime}}\hat{n}_{m^{\prime\prime}}\rangle_{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M\sum_{k=0}^{N}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{N^{\prime}}p_{k,k^{\prime}}k^{2}(k-1)k^{\prime}+\frac{M}{2}(M-1)\sum_{k=0}^{N}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{N^{\prime}}\sum_{k^{\prime\prime}=0}^{N^{\prime\prime}}p_{k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}}k(k-1)k^{\prime}k^{\prime\prime},

where
p_{k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}}=\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M-3}{N-k-k^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime}}\hskip-2.5pt\big)/\big(\hskip-2.5pt\binom{M}{N}\hskip-2.5pt\big)
, which can be approximated to
p_{k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}}\approx a_{3}\lambda_{3}^{(k+k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime})}
. Carrying out these summations using Wolfram Mathematica, assuming
M=cN^{2}
, and calculating the limit at large
N
yields to
\langle V^{2}\rangle_{u}=(3/2c+9/4c^{2})\Gamma^{2}_{\textrm{ tb}}/4
.

Figure 32:
Wolfram Mathematica code to calculate the summatories.

Further Considerations on Quantum Sensing with NV Centers

Appendix 10.A
Finding
\Omega(t)
from
F(t)

The term representing the MW driving in Eq. (
99
) is
\frac{\Omega(t)}{2}(|1\rangle\langle 0|e^{i\phi}+\textrm{ H.c.})
, and the associated propagator for, e.g., the
m
th
\pi
-pulse is

U_{t}=\exp{[-i\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{m}+t_{\pi}}\frac{\Omega(s)}{2}(|1\rangle\langle 0|e^{i\phi}+\textrm{ H.c.})\ ds]}.

(221)

During the
m
th
\pi
-pulse, i.e. in a certain time between
t_{m}
and
t_{m}+t_{\pi}
,
U_{t}
has the following effect on the electron spin
\sigma_{z}
operator (in the following,
\sigma_{\phi}=|1\rangle\langle 0|e^{i\phi}+\textrm{ H.c.}
)

\displaystyle e^{i\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{m}+t}\frac{\Omega(s)}{2}\sigma_{\phi}\ ds}\sigma_{z}e^{-i\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{m}+t}\frac{\Omega(s)}{2}\sigma_{\phi}\ ds}=e^{\big(i\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{m}+t}\Omega(s)\ ds\big)\sigma_{\phi}}\sigma_{z}

\displaystyle=\cos{\bigg(\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{m}+t}\Omega(s)\ ds\bigg)}\sigma_{z}+i\sin{\bigg(\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{m}+t}\Omega(s)\ ds\bigg)}\sigma_{\phi}\sigma_{z}.

(222)

In this manner, it is cleat that
F(t)=\cos{\big(\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{m}+t}\Omega(s)\ ds\big)}
. The other spin component, i.e. the one going with
\sin{\big(\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{m}+t}\Omega(s)\ ds\big)}
, does not participate in the joint NV-nucleus dynamics for sequences with alternating pulses
[
422
]
such as the XY8
\equiv
XYXYYXYX pulse sequence we are using in chapter
5
. A valid argument to neglect this term is that its period is twice the period of the pulse sequence, i.e.
2T
, and, thus, it is kept off resonance. Now, assuming that
F(t)
and
\Omega(t)
are differentiable, one can invert the expression
F(t)=\cos{\big(\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{m}+t}\Omega(s)\ ds\big)}
and find

\Omega(t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial_{t}}\arccos[F(t)]=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-F(t)^{2}}}.

(223)

The latter corresponds to Eq. (
109
).

Appendix 10.B
Calculation of
f_{l}
coefficients

10.B.1
Coefficients for extended pulses

The analytical expression for the coefficients
f_{l}
is given by

f_{l}=\frac{2}{T}\int_{0}^{T}F(s)\cos{\Big(\frac{2\pi ls}{T}\Big)}\ ds,

(224)

where
T=2\pi/\omega_{\textrm{ M}}
. With a rescaling of the integrating variable given by
s=xT/2
, this is rewritten as

f_{l}=\int_{0}^{2}F(x)\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx.

(225)

The function inside the integral is symmetric or antisymmetric with respect to
x=1
, depending on
l
been odd or even. This can be easily demonstrated by using
F(x+1)=-F(x)
and
\cos{[\pi l(x+1)]}=\cos(\pi l)\cos{\pi lx}
. Thus, if
l
is even and the function is symmetric with respect to
x=1
, the value of the integral will be zero. Anyway, one can work a general expression for Eq. (
225
). First, we can divide the integral in two parts,

f_{l}=\int_{0}^{1}F(x)\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx+\int_{1}^{2}F(x)\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx,

(226)

and substitute
x
for
x+1
in the second integral. Using the symmetry properties specified above, the equation reduces to

f_{l}=(1-\cos{(\pi l)})\int_{0}^{1}F(x)\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx.

(227)

Now, from
x=0
to
1
,
F(x)
can be divided in three parts defined by
\tau_{m}\equiv 2t_{m}/T
and
1-\tau_{m}
, the times corresponding to the beginning and the end of the pulse,

\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}F(x)\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx+\int_{\tau_{m}}^{1-\tau_{m}}F(x)\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx+\int_{1-\tau_{m}}^{1}F(x)\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx,

(228)

and the integral in the middle is zero for the extended pulses. This leaves us with the first and third integrals for which
F(x)
is
1
and
-1
respectively, obtaining

f_{l}^{\textrm{ m}}=(1-\cos{(\pi l)})\Bigg\{\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx-\int_{1-\tau_{m}}^{1}\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx\Bigg\},

(229)

that leads to

f_{l}^{\textrm{ m}}=\frac{1}{\pi l}(1-\cos{(\pi l)})\Bigg\{\sin{(\pi l\tau_{m})}+\sin{(\pi l(1-\tau_{m}))}\Bigg\}.

(230)

Using
\sin{[\pi l(1-\tau_{m})]}=-\sin{(\pi l\tau_{m})}\cos(\pi l)
and
\sin^{2}{\theta}=(1-\cos{(2\theta)})/2
, the expression for
f_{l}^{\textrm{ m}}
reduces to

f_{l}^{\textrm{ m}}=\frac{4}{\pi l}\sin^{4}{(\pi l/2)}\sin{(\pi l\tau_{m})}.

(231)

Now, by using the relation
T=4t_{m}+2t_{\pi}
,
f_{l}^{\textrm{ m}}
becomes

f_{l}^{\textrm{ m}}=\frac{4}{\pi l}\sin^{4}{(\pi l/2)}\sin{\Big(\pi l\Big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{t_{\pi}}{T}\Big)\Big)},

(232)

where
t_{\pi}
is the duration of a
\pi
-pulse. Eq. (
232
) is equivalent to Eq. (
110
) in the main text. To prove that, one may use the trigonometric identity
\sin(\theta+\pi l/2)=\sin{(\theta)}\cos{(\pi l/2)}+\cos{(\theta)}\sin{(\pi l/2)}
which leads us to

f_{l}^{\textrm{ m}}=\frac{4}{\pi l}\cos{\Bigg(\pi\frac{t_{\pi}}{T/l}\Bigg)}\sin{(\pi l/2)},

(233)

as
\sin^{4}{(\pi l/2)}\cos{(\pi l/2)}=0
and
\sin^{5}{(\pi l/2)}=\sin{(\pi l/2)}
.

Figure 33:
Plot of
F(x)
and
\cos{(\pi lx)}
(where
l=13
) functions between
x=0
and
x=2
, corresponding to
t=0
and
t=T
respectively.

10.B.2
Coefficients for top-hat pulses

For calculating the value of
f_{l}
coefficients in the case of top-hat pulses, we just need to sum the contribution of the second integral on Eq. (
228
), which is not zero for top-hat pulses. The value of
F(s)
during the pulse is
F(s)=\cos{[\pi(s-t_{m})/t_{\pi}]}
, where
t_{p}=t_{m}+t_{\pi}/2
. With the rescaling of the integrating variable introduced in the previous section this is rewritten as

F(s)=\cos{[\pi(x-\tau_{m})/\tau_{\pi}]},

(234)

where
\tau_{\pi}=2t_{\pi}/T
. So, we need to solve the following integral

\int_{\tau_{m}}^{1-\tau_{m}}F(x)\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx=\int_{\tau_{m}}^{1-\tau_{m}}\cos{[\pi(x-\tau_{m})/\tau_{\pi}]}\cos{(\pi lx)}\ dx

(235)

which is not zero. To solve the integral, we can displace the reference frame by a factor of
\tau_{p}=1/2
, by the change of variable
x=y+\tau_{m}+\tau_{\pi}/2=y+1/2
. Now, the integral will be centered at zero and will look like

\displaystyle\int_{-\tau_{\pi}/2}^{\tau_{\pi}/2}\cos{[\pi y/\tau_{\pi}+\pi/2]}\cos{[\pi l(y+1/2)]}\ dy

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\tau_{\pi}/2}^{\tau_{\pi}/2}\sin{(\pi y/\tau_{\pi})}\cos{[\pi l(y+1/2)]}\ dy,

(236)

which using
\cos[\pi l(y+1/2)]=\cos{(\pi ly)}\cos{(\pi l/2)}-\sin{(\pi ly)}\sin{(\pi l/2)}
becomes

\displaystyle\sin{(\pi l/2)}\int_{-\tau_{\pi}/2}^{\tau_{\pi}/2}\sin{(\pi y/\tau_{\pi})}\sin{(\pi ly)}\ dy

\displaystyle-\cos{(\pi l/2)}\int_{-\tau_{\pi}/2}^{\tau_{\pi}/2}\sin{(\pi y/\tau_{\pi})}\cos{(\pi ly)}\ dy.

(237)

The second integral is zero owing to symmetry reasons, i. e.
\int_{-a}^{a}F(x)dx=0
if
F(-x)=-F(x)
. Again, because of symmetry arguments, the first integral is

2\sin{(\pi l/2)}\int_{0}^{\tau_{\pi}/2}\sin{(\pi y/\tau_{\pi})}\sin{(\pi ly)}\ dy,

(238)

which using trigonometric identities reads

\sin{(\pi l/2)}\Big\{\int_{0}^{\tau_{\pi}/2}\cos{(\pi y(l-1/\tau_{\pi})}\ dy-\int_{0}^{\tau_{\pi}/2}\cos{(\pi y(l+1/\tau_{\pi})}\ dy.\Big\}

(239)

Solving the integrals one gets

\frac{-1}{\pi}\sin{(\pi l/2)}\cos{(\pi l\tau_{\pi}/2)}\Big\{\frac{1}{l-1/\tau_{\pi}}+\frac{1}{l+1/\tau_{\pi}}\Big\},

(240)

which is simplified to

\frac{2l\tau_{\pi}^{2}}{\pi(1-l^{2}\tau_{\pi}^{2})}\sin{(\pi l/2)}\cos{(\pi l\tau_{\pi}/2)}.

(241)

It is straightforward to prove that the sum of the three integrals in Eq. (
228
) gives

f_{l}^{\textrm{ th}}=\frac{4\sin{(\pi l/2)}\cos{(\pi lt_{\pi}/T)}}{\pi l(1-4l^{2}t_{\pi}^{2}/T^{2})},

(242)

which correspond to the expression written in the main text.

Appendix 10.C
Energy delivery

The Poynting vector, that describes the energy flux for an electromagnetic wave, is given by

\vec{P}=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\vec{E}\times\vec{B},

(243)

where
\mu_{0}
is the vacuum permeability, and
\vec{E}
and
\vec{B}
are the electric field and magnetic field vectors at the region of interest, i.e. the NV center. The latter, in the nanoscale, is sufficiently small compared with the wavelength of the MW radiation to assume a plane wave description of the radiation, so the magnetic field can be written as

\vec{B}=\vec{B}_{0}(t)\cos{(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}-\omega t+\phi)},

(244)

where
\vec{k}
is the wavevector and
\omega
the frequency of the microwave field. We will also assume an extra time dependence
B_{0}(t)
whose time scales will be several orders of magnitude larger than the period
2\pi/\omega
. From Maxwell equations in vacuum it is derived that, for such a magnetic field,
\vec{k}\cdot\vec{B}=0
,
\vec{k}\cdot\vec{E}=0
, and
\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}=0
. From the equation
\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B}=\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partial\vec{E}/\partial t
, it follows that

\vec{E}=c^{2}\int\!dt\ (\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B})=-c^{2}\int\!dt\ (\vec{k}\times\vec{B_{0}}(t))\sin{(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}-\omega t+\phi)}.

(245)

We choose
\vec{B}
to be perpendicular to the NV axis (
z
axis), specifically, on the
x
axis. The control Hamiltonian, is then

H_{c}(t)=-\gamma_{e}\vec{B}\cdot\vec{S}=\gamma_{e}B_{x}(t)S_{x}\cos{(\omega t-\phi)},

(246)

where
\vec{S}
corresponds to the spin of the NV center,
\gamma_{e}
is the gyromagnetic ratio of the electron and
\vec{x}=0
the position of the NV. To recover Eq. (
98
) of the main text, we require that
\sqrt{2}\Omega(t)=\gamma_{e}B_{x}(t)
. The magnetic field vector at
\vec{x}=0
is then

\vec{B}(t)=\frac{\sqrt{2}\Omega(t)}{\gamma_{e}}\cos{(\omega t-\phi)}\hat{x}\\

(247)

and the electric field is, from Eq. (
245
),

\vec{E}(t)=\frac{\sqrt{2}\omega c}{\gamma_{e}}\int dt\Omega(t)\sin{(\omega t-\phi)}\ \hat{k}\times\hat{x},

(248)

which, using the wave equation
\partial^{2}\vec{E}/\partial^{2}t=c^{2}\nabla^{2}\vec{E}=\omega^{2}\vec{E}
, converts into

\vec{E}(t)=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{k\gamma_{e}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big[\Omega(t)\sin{(\omega t-\phi)}\Big]\ \hat{x}\times\hat{k}.

(249)

10.C.1
The case of top-hat
\pi
pulses

For top-hat pulses we have that
\partial\Omega(t)/\partial t=0
during the pulse, thus, the energy delivery per unit of area we obtain for top-hat pulses is

E^{\textrm{ th}}(t_{\pi})=\int_{0}^{t_{\pi}}dt|\vec{P}(t)|=\frac{c}{\mu_{0}}\frac{2}{\gamma_{e}^{2}}\int_{0}^{t_{\pi}}dt\ \Omega^{2}\cos^{2}(\omega t-\phi)

(250)

which gives

E^{\textrm{ th}}(t_{\pi})=\frac{c}{\mu_{0}}\frac{\Omega^{2}}{\gamma_{e}^{2}}\Big\{t_{\pi}+\frac{1}{2\omega}\sin{(2\omega t_{\pi}-2\phi)}\Big\}.

(251)

The second part of the formula is upper bounded by
(2\omega)^{-1}
, which , on the other hand, is several orders of magnitude smaller than
t_{\pi}
, thus negligible. As
t_{\pi}=\pi/\Omega
, Eq. (
251
) can be rewritten as

E^{\textrm{ th}}(t_{\pi})\approx\frac{\pi c}{\mu_{0}}\frac{\Omega}{\gamma_{e}^{2}},

(252)

meaning that the energy increases linearly with the Rabi frequency.

10.C.2
The case of extended
\pi
pulses

To study the case of an extended
\pi
pulse, we need to calculate both terms on Eq. (
249
), which are non zero in general. The complete expression is given by

E^{\textrm{ ext}}(t_{\pi})=\frac{c}{\mu_{0}}\frac{2}{\gamma_{e}^{2}}\int_{0}^{t_{\pi}}\!\!dt\ \Bigg[\Omega^{2}(t)\cos^{2}{(\omega t-\phi)}+\frac{1}{\omega}\Omega(t)\frac{\partial\Omega(t)}{\partial t}\cos{(\omega t-\phi)}\sin{(\omega t-\phi)}\Bigg].

(253)

As a final comment, for all cases simulated in the main text we find that the second term at the right hand side of Eq. (
253
) is negligible, thus it can be written

E^{\textrm{ ext}}(t_{\pi})\approx\frac{c}{\mu_{0}}\frac{2}{\gamma_{e}^{2}}\int_{0}^{t_{\pi}}\!\!dt\ \Bigg[\Omega^{2}(t)\cos^{2}{(\omega t-\phi)}\Bigg].

(254)

10.C.3
Equivalent top-hat Rabi frequency

To calculate the constant Rabi frequency leading to top-hat pulses with the same energy than extended pulses, one has to equal
E^{\textrm{ top-hat}}(t_{\pi})=E^{\textrm{ ext}}(t_{\pi})
and extract the value of the constant
\Omega
. With Eqs. (
252
,
253
) one can easily find that

\Omega=\frac{\mu_{0}\gamma_{e}^{2}}{\pi c}E^{\textrm{ ext}}(t_{\pi}).

(255)
